C0001  extended reality (XR)  [defined; defined_in: 1/S1, 2/S0; 40 of 51 facts shown]
  Extended reality (XR) is an umbrella term, not a specific technology, that covers all present or future immersive technologies, such as augmented reality (AR), virtual reality (VR), and mixed reality (MR).
  built from: [3] 1/S1/F0

C0002  virtual reality (VR)  [defined; defined_in: 4/S0, 5/S1, 6/S1, 8/S0, 10/S1, 25/S1, 60/S3; 40 of 328 facts shown]
  Virtual reality (VR) is an artificial environment experienced through sensory stimuli provided by a computer, in which a person's actions partially determine what happens and which replaces the user's sense of reality without requiring the physical existence of objects or places.
  built from: [20] 4/S0/F2, [21] 5/S1/F0, [22] 5/S1/F1, [26] 8/S0/F0

C0003  augmented reality (AR)  [defined; defined_in: 1/S2, 3/S0, 5/S0, 5/S1, 5/S2, 60/S3; 40 of 183 facts shown]
  Augmented reality (AR) is an enhanced version of reality created by technology, in which computer-generated digital content is overlaid on the real world in real time and registered in 3D, so the user perceives and interacts with the enhanced environment.
  built from: [27] 5/S0/F0, [28] 5/S0/F1, [29] 5/S0/F2

C0004  mixed reality (MR)  [defined; defined_in: 1/S2, 5/S2, 60/S3; 21 of 21 facts shown]
  Mixed reality (MR) is an immersive technology that is the same as augmented reality, in which digital content is overlaid on the view of reality and can be interacted with.
  built from: [3] 1/S1/F0, [14] 5/S2/F5, [15] 5/S2/F6

C0005  head-mounted display (headset, HMDs, HMD, headsets, VR headset, head-mounted displays)  [defined; defined_in: 6/S2, 14/S2; 40 of 67 facts shown]
  A head-mounted display (HMD) is a wearable, mobile visual display that integrates electronic, optical, mechanical, and audio components and uses one or two small screens directly in front of the user's eyes to place images for monoscopic or stereoscopic viewing. It can be non-see-through, optical see-through, or video see-through.
  built from: [35] 14/S2/F3, [37] 14/S2/F5, [38] 14/S2/F6, [39] 14/S2/F7

C0837  drift correction  [defined; defined_in: 16/S3, 17/S3; 5 of 5 facts shown]
  Drift correction is a step in the tracking process that compensates for the difference between estimated and actual position and orientation of the user, sense organs, body parts, or objects in the environment, reducing estimation error. It is usually done by blending values from at least two sensors, such as a gyroscope and camera, to estimate and correct drift.
  built from: [1] 16/S3/F1, [2] 16/S3/F5, [3] 17/S3/F0, [4] 17/S3/F1

  Drift error is an error in inertial measurement unit (IMU) sensor tracking that can be detected using accelerometer and magnetometer readings.
  built from: [1] 17/S0/F2

C0844  measured angular velocity  [defined; defined_in: none; 6 of 6 facts shown]
  Measured angular velocity is the angular velocity value produced by an inertial measurement unit (IMU) sensor, which can differ from the actual angular velocity because the measurement is imperfect and is transformed by calibration into a value that is the closest possible match to it.
  built from: [1] 17/S1/F0, [4] 17/S1/F3, [6] 17/S1/F5
  checks: defined_mismatch

C0845  actual angular velocity  [not defined; defined_in: none; 6 of 6 facts shown]
  Actual angular velocity is the angular velocity value closely approximated by a highly accurate benchmark sensor in the calibration of inertial measurement unit sensors, and the value that measured and calibrated readings are made to match as closely as possible.
  built from: [2] 17/S1/F1, [4] 17/S1/F3, [6] 17/S1/F5

C0846  measurement error  [defined; defined_in: 17/S1; 2 of 2 facts shown]
  Measurement error is the difference between measured angular velocity and actual angular velocity in the calibration of inertial measurement unit (IMU) sensors, caused by imperfect devices and measurement technique.
  built from: [1] 17/S1/F0

C0847  calibration model  [defined; defined_in: none; 1 of 1 facts shown]
  A calibration model is a model that assumes a relationship between measured angular velocity and actual angular velocity; one simple model assumes a linear relationship, though other relationships are possible.
  built from: [1] 17/S1/F2
  checks: defined_mismatch

C0848  linear relationship  [not defined; defined_in: none; 1 of 1 facts shown]
  A Linear relationship is a relationship that a simple calibration model can assume between measured angular velocity and actual angular velocity, but it is not the only possible relationship.
  built from: [1] 17/S1/F2

C0849  paired samples  [defined; defined_in: 17/S1; 2 of 2 facts shown]
  Paired samples are pairs of measurements, one from an inertial measurement unit and one from a benchmark sensor, used in calibration to compare measured values with actual values.
  built from: [1] 17/S1/F3, [2] 17/S1/F4

C0850  benchmark sensor  [defined; defined_in: none; 1 of 1 facts shown]
  A benchmark sensor is an expensive, highly accurate sensor that produces the actual values, very close rather than exact or ideal, used in calibration with measured values from a less accurate sensor.
  built from: [1] 17/S1/F3
  checks: defined_mismatch

C0851  linear least squares regression  [defined; defined_in: 17/S1; 1 of 1 facts shown]
  Linear least squares regression is a classical optimization technique that finds constants for a linear expression by minimizing the sum of the squared differences between the expression and actual values.
  built from: [1] 17/S1/F4

C0852  calibrated value  [defined; defined_in: 17/S1; 1 of 1 facts shown]
  Calibrated value is a value that calibration transforms from an inexpensive measured angular velocity into the closest possible match with the actual value, using omega_cal = C1 + C2 omega_g.
  built from: [1] 17/S1/F5

C0853  initial orientation (initial pose)  [defined; defined_in: 17/S2; 3 of 3 facts shown]
  Initial orientation is the starting orientation of a sensor or user, found during registration to set the initial alignment between the real and virtual worlds. In a simplifying assumption, it is taken as the headset's facing direction when tracking starts, with the initial alignment set to the forward direction.
  built from: [1] 17/S2/F0, [2] 17/S2/F1

C0854  initial alignment  [defined; defined_in: 17/S2; 2 of 2 facts shown]
  Initial alignment is the alignment between the real and virtual worlds set by registration from the initial pose or orientation of the sensor or user; as a simplifying assumption, it is set to the forward direction when the tracking system is turned on.
  built from: [1] 17/S2/F0, [2] 17/S2/F1

C0858  current sensor orientation  [defined; defined_in: 17/S2; 1 of 1 facts shown]
  Current sensor orientation is the sensor orientation at the current time, computed by adding the sum of calibrated angular velocity samples multiplied by the sampling interval over the elapsed samples to the initial orientation estimated during registration.
  built from: [1] 17/S2/F3

C0859  calibrated angular velocity sample  [defined; defined_in: 17/S2; 2 of 2 facts shown]
  A calibrated angular velocity sample is an angular velocity sample from a gyroscopic sensor at an instant that indicates a change in orientation equal to the sample multiplied by the sampling interval, and is used to compute the sensor orientation.
  built from: [1] 17/S2/F3, [2] 17/S2/F4

C0860  sampling interval  [defined; defined_in: none; 2 of 2 facts shown]
  Sampling interval is the usually regular, discrete time interval between samples in the sensor data stream, used to multiply angular velocity samples to compute orientation changes.

C0862  angular velocity sample  [defined; defined_in: none; 1 of 1 facts shown]

C0864  drift  [not defined; defined_in: none; 1 of 1 facts shown]

C0865  drift-corrected orientation  [defined; defined_in: 17/S3; 1 of 1 facts shown]
  Drift-corrected orientation is an orientation resulting from a drift-correction approach that combines, at the same time instant, an orientation obtained by integrating gyroscope values and an orientation obtained with a camera, using a gain parameter alpha between 0 and 1 so that the gyroscope orientation is weighted by alpha and the camera orientation by 1 minus alpha.
  built from: [1] 17/S3/F2

C0866  gain parameter (alpha)  [defined; defined_in: 17/S3; 1 of 1 facts shown]
  Gain parameter is a normalized value between 0 and 1 used in a classic drift-correction approach to combine a gyroscope-derived orientation with a camera-derived orientation.
  built from: [1] 17/S3/F2

C1930  pointing-based selection  [defined; defined_in: 39/S3; 6 of 6 facts shown]

C1931  image plane selection (occlusion, framing)  [defined; defined_in: 39/S4; 6 of 6 facts shown]
  Image plane selection is a two-dimensional selection technique in which the user selects on a view plane that contains the object as viewed from a distance and holds one or two hands or fingers between the eye and the object and provides a signal such as a gesture or hand movement when the object lines up.
  built from: [3] 39/S4/F1, [4] 39/S4/F2

C1932  volumetric selection (volume-based selection)  [defined; defined_in: 39/S4; 6 of 6 facts shown]

C1934  hand selection technique  [defined; defined_in: 39/S2; 6 of 6 facts shown]

  built from: [1] 39/S2/F0

C1935  grab  [not defined; defined_in: none; 1 of 1 facts shown]
  Grab is part of a hand selection technique that selects virtual objects in a virtual world by having the user directly reach out to virtually touch the object.

C1936  go-go technique (go-go)  [defined; defined_in: 39/S2; 6 of 6 facts shown]
  The Go-go technique is a virtual hand selection technique for distant virtual objects that uses one-to-one mapping when the target is close, allows the user to change virtual arm and hand length interactively, and, as distance increases, maps smaller physical hand movements to larger and faster virtual hand movements, possibly exponentially, so distant objects can be reached quickly.
  built from: [1] 39/S2/F1, [2] 39/S2/F4

C1937  prism technique (prism)  [defined; defined_in: 39/S2; 5 of 5 facts shown]
  Prism technique is a travel technique for hand selection of virtual objects that applies scaled-down motion to the virtual hand when the user's physical hand moves below a specified speed, and keeps the virtual hand still when the hand velocity is below a minimum velocity.
  built from: [1] 39/S2/F1, [2] 39/S2/F6, [5] 39/S2/F11

C1938  intent-driven selection  [defined; defined_in: 39/S2; 4 of 4 facts shown]
  Intent-driven selection is a grabbing hand selection technique in which a user moves a virtual hand toward an object and either positions virtual fingers or uses their posture around it to indicate the confidence level in selecting it, typically using proximity spheres that encompass multiple objects and reduce in size to refine the selection to a specific object.
  built from: [2] 39/S2/F9, [3] 39/S2/F10, [4] 39/S2/F11

C1939  one-to-one mapping  [defined; defined_in: none; 3 of 3 facts shown]
  One-to-one mapping is a mapping of a physical hand's motion and orientation to a virtual hand, tying the virtual hand to the physical hand and allowing natural hand movements.
  built from: [1] 39/S2/F2
  checks: defined_mismatch

  built from: [1] 39/S2/F4

C1941  hand velocity  [not defined; defined_in: none; 1 of 1 facts shown]
  Hand velocity is the speed of the user's physical hand, used to decide when the virtual hand remains still in the prism technique.
  built from: [1] 39/S2/F6

C1942  proximity sphere  [defined; defined_in: 39/S2; 1 of 1 facts shown]
  A Proximity sphere is a selection area used by intent-driven selection techniques that encompasses multiple objects at a larger distance and progressively reduces in size, depending on hand or finger postures, as the user moves closer, refining the selection to a specific object.
  built from: [1] 39/S2/F10

C1950  bend cast  [defined; defined_in: 39/S3; 2 of 2 facts shown]
  Bend cast is an improvement over basic ray casting in which the system bends the ray toward the closest selectable object, detected by calculating the point-line distance from each selectable object, so the user need not point precisely; a circular arc can provide visual feedback for the bending.
  built from: [1] 39/S3/F6

  built from: [1] 39/S3/F6

C1952  point-line distance  [not defined; defined_in: none; 1 of 1 facts shown]

  built from: [1] 39/S3/F6

C1953  depth ray  [defined; defined_in: 39/S3; 1 of 1 facts shown]
  Depth ray is an improved ray-casting technique that uses a user-controlled depth marker, positioned by moving the hand forward or backward, to disambiguate multiple intersected objects and select the object intersected by the pointing vector and closest to the marker.
  built from: [1] 39/S3/F8

C1985  world in miniature (WIM, world in miniature metaphor)  [defined; defined_in: 39/S6, 40/S4; 5 of 5 facts shown]

Manipulating a model object manipulates the corresponding virtual object, even if it is outside the user's reach; moving certain model objects can also change the virtual viewpoint.

  built from: [1] 39/S7/F4

C1995  integrated hybrid manipulation technique  [defined; defined_in: 39/S8; 2 of 2 facts shown]
  An Integrated hybrid manipulation technique is a technique that automatically switches from selection to manipulation after selection, without an explicit user signal, and switches back to selection mode after manipulation, so selection and manipulation are not performed separately.
  built from: [1] 39/S8/F0, [2] 39/S8/F1

C1996  Hand-Centered Object Manipulation Extending Ray Casting (HOMER)  [defined; defined_in: 39/S8; 4 of 4 facts shown]
  Hand-Centered Object Manipulation Extending Ray Casting (HOMER) is an integrated hybrid manipulation technique in which the user selects an object by ray casting, the virtual hand automatically moves to and attaches to the object instead of the ray attaching, and hand manipulation changes the object’s position and orientation until the user ends it with a trigger or gesture.
  built from: [1] 39/S8/F1, [2] 39/S8/F2, [3] 39/S8/F3, [4] 39/S8/F4

C1997  wayfinding (wayfinding part)  [defined; defined_in: 40/S5; 13 of 13 facts shown]
  Wayfinding is the component of navigation, other than locomotion or travel, that provides location awareness, ascertains a path to a destination, transfers spatial knowledge to the real world, and builds a mental model of the environment.
  built from: [2] 40/S5/F0, [3] 40/S5/F1, [4] 40/S5/F2

C1998  exploration  [defined; defined_in: 40/S1; 1 of 1 facts shown]
  Exploration is a kind of travel in navigation that has no explicit goal or target point, where the user moves around to see, learn, or explore.
  built from: [1] 40/S1/F2

C1999  search  [defined; defined_in: 40/S1; 1 of 1 facts shown]
  Search is a way of traveling in navigation that involves moving to a specified target location.
  built from: [1] 40/S1/F2

C2000  maneuvering  [defined; defined_in: 40/S1; 1 of 1 facts shown]
  Maneuvering is a type of travel in navigation that comprises short, precise movements that change the viewpoint or break a path into small segments to reach a destination.
  built from: [1] 40/S1/F2

C2001  walking-based locomotion  [defined; defined_in: 40/S2; 4 of 4 facts shown]
  Walking-based locomotion is a locomotion technique for traveling in a virtual environment in which physical walking or movement in the physical world is mapped to corresponding movement in the virtual environment, and it is broadly divided into three categories: full gait techniques, partial gait techniques, and gait negation techniques.
  built from: [1] 40/S1/F3, [3] 40/S2/F0, [4] 40/S2/F1

C2002  selection-based locomotion  [defined; defined_in: 40/S4; 5 of 5 facts shown]
  Selection-based locomotion is a category of locomotion technique in which the user selects where to go rather than continuously specifying how to move there by steering or looking. Its two types are target selection-based locomotion and route planning-based locomotion; route planning-based locomotion has the user select a route to the target rather than only the endpoint.
  built from: [3] 40/S4/F0, [4] 40/S4/F1, [5] 40/S4/F4

C2003  manipulation-based locomotion  [defined; defined_in: 40/S1, 40/S4; 4 of 4 facts shown]
  Manipulation-based locomotion is a type of locomotion technique in which the user's position, orientation, or scale is manipulated using gestures that control either the virtual viewpoint or the virtual world.
  built from: [1] 40/S1/F3, [3] 40/S4/F5

C2004  full gait technique  [defined; defined_in: 40/S2; 3 of 3 facts shown]
  Full gait technique is a walking-based locomotion technique that is the most natural way to travel and in which the user's actual real-world walking, restricted within a specified physical limit, is translated into any amount of virtual movement in a potentially infinite virtual space, creating a mismatch between physical and virtual movement.
  built from: [1] 40/S2/F1, [2] 40/S2/F2, [3] 40/S2/F3

C2005  partial gait technique  [defined; defined_in: 40/S2; 3 of 3 facts shown]
  A Partial gait technique is a walking-based locomotion technique in which the user walks in place without changing position, requiring specialized devices to map the pattern of walking in a single place to different types of walking in the virtual space.
  built from: [1] 40/S2/F1, [2] 40/S2/F4, [3] 40/S2/F5

C2006  gait negation technique  [defined; defined_in: 40/S2; 4 of 4 facts shown]
  A Gait negation technique is a walking-based virtual locomotion technique that requires a specialized setup to cancel the user's lateral movements and keep the user's physical position fixed before the movement is mapped to virtual navigation.
  built from: [1] 40/S2/F1, [2] 40/S2/F6, [4] 49/S2/F1

C2007  virtual navigation  [not defined; defined_in: none; 2 of 2 facts shown]

  built from: [1] 40/S2/F3, [2] 40/S2/F6

C2016  steering-based navigation  [defined; defined_in: 40/S3; 2 of 2 facts shown]
  Steering-based navigation is a navigation category in which direction and speed are continuously controlled while the user moves from one point to another. Its two types are spatial steering-based navigation, controlled by the user's body parts, and physical steering-based navigation, controlled by vehicular props or additional hardware.
  built from: [1] 40/S3/F0, [2] 40/S3/F1

  built from: [1] 40/S3/F1, [2] 40/S3/F2

C2138  interface sketch (sketch, single interface sketch)  [defined; defined_in: 42/S4; 5 of 5 facts shown]

A single drawing gives a horizontal prototype, whereas a series can give a vertical prototype of interaction.

C2177  scenario (task description)  [defined; defined_in: none; 1 of 1 facts shown]

  checks: defined_mismatch

C2178  interface-level task (interface-level tasks)  [defined; defined_in: 43/S2; 1 of 1 facts shown]

C2179  usability question (usability questions)  [defined; defined_in: none; 2 of 2 facts shown]
  A usability question is a question framed before a cognitive walkthrough to reflect usability issues; evaluators use it as a guideline to identify likely user problems and must answer it with a detailed report rather than a simple yes-or-no response.
  built from: [1] 43/S2/F4, [2] 43/S2/F5
  checks: defined_mismatch

C2180  report (evaluators' reports)  [defined; defined_in: none; 2 of 2 facts shown]
  A report is a detailed answer that evaluators give for each usability question, rather than a simple yes or no answer, explaining where difficulty was felt in an interface or interaction and why, and it is collected, combined, and analyzed to identify broader usability issues.

C2190  Nielsen's ten heuristics (Nielsen's 10 heuristics)  [defined; defined_in: 43/S3; 3 of 3 facts shown]
  Nielsen's ten heuristics is a general set of usability heuristics, primarily for graphical user interfaces but applicable to most interactive systems. It consists of: visibility of system status, real-world match, user control, consistency, error prevention, recognition over recall, flexibility, minimal design, error recovery, and help and documentation. It is used to identify usability problems early from low-fidelity prototypes.
  built from: [1] 43/S3/F2, [2] 43/S3/F3, [3] 43/S3/F4

C2296  within-subject design (repeated measures design)  [defined; defined_in: 46/S2; 7 of 7 facts shown]

  built from: [1] 46/S2/F2

C2299  confounding variable  [not defined; defined_in: none; 1 of 1 facts shown]

C2491  travel-time minimisation problem  [defined; defined_in: 51/S5; 4 of 4 facts shown]
  Travel-time minimisation problem is a graph problem, a variation of the travelling salesman problem, whose objective is to find a path containing all vertices with the minimum total travel time. The graph is connected and not assumed to be a complete graph; for vertices not directly connected, a hypothetical edge is weighted by the shortest path length.

  built from: [2] 51/S5/F2

C2606  hidden surface removal  [defined; defined_in: 57/S4, 58/S6; 8 of 8 facts shown]

C2614  three-dimensional object representation technique (3D object representation technique)  [not defined; defined_in: none; 2 of 2 facts shown]

C2615  point sample rendering  [defined; defined_in: 55/S3; 5 of 5 facts shown]
  Point sample rendering is the simplest broad category of three-dimensional object representation techniques, in which scene information is captured from the real world as raw data points on the scene, each carrying attributes, and the points are processed or mapped to pixels to generate a synthetic image, relying on real-world information rather than synthetically defining objects.
  built from: [1] 55/S2/F3, [3] 55/S3/F1, [4] 55/S3/F2, [5] 55/S3/F3

C2616  boundary representation  [defined; defined_in: 55/S2, 55/S3; 6 of 6 facts shown]
  Boundary representation is a broad category of three-dimensional object representation techniques in which objects are represented by the surfaces that define their boundary, with those surfaces being polygonal or curved. It comprises mesh, parametric, and implicit forms, with mesh as the most basic form.
  built from: [1] 55/S2/F3, [3] 55/S3/F4, [6] 55/S4/F6

C2617  space partitioning based representation (space partitioning-based representation, space partitioning representation)  [defined; defined_in: 55/S2, 55/S5; 6 of 6 facts shown]
  Space partitioning based representation is a broad category of 3D object representation in which the space occupied by an object is divided into disjoint, non-overlapping regions, so any point inside the object lies in exactly one region and the object is represented by the occupied regions.
  built from: [1] 55/S2/F3, [3] 55/S5/F0

C2618  mesh (mesh representation, polygonal mesh, meshes, 3D mesh)  [defined; defined_in: 55/S3, 61/S1; 12 of 12 facts shown]
  Mesh is a basic boundary representation technique in computer graphics that represents a three-dimensional object's surface as connected polygons, usually triangles, and stores the vertices and connections needed to reconstruct it.
  built from: [1] 55/S2/F3, [2] 55/S3/F5, [3] 55/S3/F6, [5] 55/S3/F8

C2619  parametric representation (parametric forms)  [not defined; defined_in: none; 3 of 3 facts shown]

C2659  shearing  [defined; defined_in: 56/S2; 4 of 4 facts shown]

C2660  matrix  [not defined; defined_in: none; 2 of 2 facts shown]

C2661  homogeneous coordinate system  [defined; defined_in: 56/S3; 2 of 2 facts shown]

C2671  composite transformation matrix  [defined; defined_in: 56/S4, 57/S1; 5 of 5 facts shown]

C2680  order of application (right-to-left sequence of transformations)  [defined; defined_in: 56/S4; 2 of 2 facts shown]
  The order of application is the sequence in which individual transformations are applied, determining the placement of the transformation matrices in a composite transformation matrix: the first-applied matrix is rightmost and each later matrix is placed to the left. Because matrix multiplication is not commutative, an incorrect sequence may produce an incorrect transformed result.
  built from: [1] 56/S4/F3, [2] 56/S4/F6

C2684  shading model  [defined; defined_in: 56/S5; 3 of 3 facts shown]

C2686  continuous intensity value  [defined; defined_in: 56/S5; 1 of 1 facts shown]

C2688  surface point  [not defined; defined_in: none; 7 of 7 facts shown]
  A Surface point is a point on a surface in computer graphics at which light falls directly or, in some cases, after reflection and is reflected to a viewer's eye, the reflected intensity determining the perceived color.
  built from: [1] 56/S6/F0, [2] 56/S6/F1

C2690  simple lighting model  [defined; defined_in: 56/S6, 56/S7, 56/S8; 11 of 11 facts shown]
  Simple lighting model is a lighting model that models the light intensity reaching the viewer from a surface point as the sum of ambient light intensity and the diffuse and specular reflection intensities of direct light.
  built from: [1] 56/S6/F2

C2691  ambient light intensity (I amb)  [not defined; defined_in: none; 2 of 2 facts shown]
  Ambient light intensity is a light intensity falling on a surface point, and in a simple lighting model it is one of the three intensities summed to give the light intensity reaching a viewer.
  built from: [1] 56/S6/F2, [2] 56/S7/F3

C2692  diffuse reflection intensity (I diff)  [not defined; defined_in: none; 1 of 1 facts shown]
  Diffuse reflection intensity is one of the three light intensities in a simple lighting model, whose sum is the light intensity reaching a viewer from a surface point.
  built from: [1] 56/S6/F2

C2693  specular reflection intensity (I spec)  [not defined; defined_in: none; 1 of 1 facts shown]
  Specular reflection intensity is one of the three intensities in the simple lighting model that are summed to give the light intensity reaching the viewer from a surface point, specifically the specular reflection intensity of direct light.
  built from: [1] 56/S6/F2

C2694  direct light  [not defined; defined_in: none; 2 of 2 facts shown]
  Direct light is the light in a simple lighting model whose diffuse and specular reflection intensities are summed with ambient light intensity to give the intensity reaching a viewer from a surface point.
  built from: [1] 56/S6/F2

C2695  diffuse reflection component (diffuse light component, diffuse reflection)  [defined; defined_in: 56/S6, 56/S7; 4 of 4 facts shown]
  Diffuse reflection component is direct light reflected in a diffuse manner from a surface in a simple lighting model, computed from the diffuse coefficient, source intensity, and the dot product of surface normal and light source direction, or zero when that dot product is not positive.
  built from: [1] 56/S6/F4, [3] 56/S7/F4

C2696  specular reflection component  [defined; defined_in: 56/S6; 2 of 2 facts shown]
  Specular reflection component is light in a simple lighting model that comes from a light source and is reflected with a specular effect, producing a shiny bright spot.
  built from: [1] 56/S6/F5

C2697  reflected light intensity (intensity of light reflected)  [defined; defined_in: 56/S7; 2 of 2 facts shown]
  Reflected light intensity is the intensity of light reflected from a surface and reaching the viewer's eye, a fraction of the incident light intensity determined by the surface's reflection coefficient.
  built from: [1] 56/S7/F0

C2698  incident light intensity  [not defined; defined_in: none; 1 of 1 facts shown]
  Incident light intensity is the light intensity that, together with a surface's reflectivity, determines the intensity of light reflected from the surface and reaching the viewer's eye.
  built from: [1] 56/S7/F0

C2699  reflection coefficient (reflectivity, k, kd, ks)  [defined; defined_in: 56/S7; 5 of 5 facts shown]
  A reflection coefficient is a surface property that determines the fraction of incident light intensity reflected from a surface and reaching the viewer's eye. In the simple lighting model, the ambient, diffuse, and specular coefficients range from 0.0 to 1.0, where values near 0.0 are dull and values near 1.0 are shiny.
  built from: [1] 56/S7/F0, [2] 56/S7/F1, [3] 56/S7/F2

C2700  specular component (specular reflection)  [defined; defined_in: 56/S7; 3 of 3 facts shown]
  The specular component is the part of a simple lighting model that is computed for direct light with the Phong approximate model as Ks Is (V·r)^Ns, or 0 when V·r is not greater than 0.
  built from: [1] 56/S7/F1, [2] 56/S7/F5, [3] 56/S7/F7

C2701  ambient reflection coefficient (Ka)  [defined; defined_in: 56/S7; 2 of 2 facts shown]
  Ambient reflection coefficient (Ka) is a reflection coefficient in a simple lighting model for ambient light, used to compute the ambient component of reflected light intensity by multiplying the ambient light intensity falling on a surface point by Ka.
  built from: [1] 56/S7/F1, [2] 56/S7/F3

C2702  diffuse reflection coefficient (Kd)  [defined; defined_in: 56/S7; 2 of 2 facts shown]

  built from: [1] 56/S7/F1, [2] 56/S7/F4

C2703  specular reflection coefficient (Ks)  [defined; defined_in: 56/S7; 2 of 2 facts shown]
  Specular reflection coefficient is a reflection coefficient in a simple lighting model that is used for specular reflection of direct light.
  built from: [1] 56/S7/F1

C2704  ambient component  [defined; defined_in: 56/S7; 1 of 1 facts shown]
  The ambient component is the component of reflected light intensity computed by multiplying the ambient light intensity falling on a surface point by the reflection coefficient for ambient light.
  built from: [1] 56/S7/F3

C2705  source light intensity (Is)  [not defined; defined_in: none; 2 of 2 facts shown]
  Source light intensity is a factor used in computing the diffuse and specular reflection components of direct light.
  built from: [1] 56/S7/F4, [2] 56/S7/F7

C2706  dot product (vector dot products)  [not defined; defined_in: none; 5 of 5 facts shown]

  built from: [5] 58/S6/F6

C2707  surface normal vector (N)  [not defined; defined_in: none; 2 of 2 facts shown]
  The surface normal vector is a vector at a surface point from which light is reflected, used in computing diffuse reflection and the ideal specular reflection direction.

C2708  light source direction vector (L)  [not defined; defined_in: none; 2 of 2 facts shown]

C2709  Phong model (Phong)  [defined; defined_in: 56/S7; 1 of 1 facts shown]
  The Phong model is an approximate model used to compute the specular component.
  built from: [1] 56/S7/F5

C2710  specular angle (angle phi, phi)  [defined; defined_in: 56/S7; 1 of 1 facts shown]
  Specular angle is the angle in the specular calculation between the viewer direction vector and the ideal specular reflection direction vector, the direction a mirror would reflect the light, and its cosine is the dot product of the two vectors.
  built from: [1] 56/S7/F6

C2711  viewer direction vector (V)  [not defined; defined_in: none; 2 of 2 facts shown]
  Viewer direction vector is a direction vector used in specular calculation, where it and the ideal specular reflection direction vector form the angle phi.
  built from: [1] 56/S7/F6

C2712  ideal specular reflection direction vector (r)  [defined; defined_in: 56/S7; 3 of 3 facts shown]
  The ideal specular reflection direction vector is the direction a mirror would reflect light, equal to twice the dot product of the light source direction vector and the surface normal vector times the surface normal vector, minus the light source direction vector.
  built from: [1] 56/S7/F6, [3] 56/S7/F8

C2713  radial intensity attenuation factor (intensity attenuation factor over distance)  [defined; defined_in: 56/S8; 3 of 3 facts shown]
  Radial intensity attenuation factor is a lighting factor that accounts for the decrease in light intensity with distance from a light source to a surface point, typically computed as an inverse quadratic function of that distance using coefficients a0, a1, and a2, that can be varied for a more realistic effect.
  built from: [1] 56/S8/F0, [2] 56/S8/F1

C2714  inverse quadratic function  [not defined; defined_in: none; 1 of 1 facts shown]
  An Inverse quadratic function is a function of the distance between a light source and a surface point, used to compute the radial intensity attenuation factor, with coefficients that can be varied for a more realistic effect.
  built from: [1] 56/S8/F1

C2715  coefficient (coefficients, a0, a1, a2)  [not defined; defined_in: none; 1 of 1 facts shown]
  Coefficient is an adjustable value used in the radial intensity attenuation factor to make the diminishing of light intensity with distance more realistic.
  built from: [1] 56/S8/F1

C2716  angular intensity attenuation  [defined; defined_in: 56/S8; 2 of 2 facts shown]
  Angular intensity attenuation is an attenuation factor in a simple lighting model, used primarily to generate spotlight effects by making elements outside the spotlight region darker and by multiplying the sum of the diffuse and specular reflection components, together with radial attenuation, while leaving the I_M intensity component unchanged.
  built from: [1] 56/S8/F2, [2] 56/S8/F4

C2717  spotlight effect (spotlight effects)  [defined; defined_in: 56/S8; 1 of 1 facts shown]
  A spotlight effect is an effect in which elements inside the spotlight region are visible or brightly visible and elements outside the spotlight zone are darker.
  built from: [1] 56/S8/F2

C2718  spotlight region (spotlight zone, spotlight cone)  [defined; defined_in: 56/S8; 2 of 2 facts shown]
  Spotlight region is the angular range in a spotlight effect, defined by theta, in which elements are visible or brightly visible and outside which they are darker.
  built from: [1] 56/S8/F2, [2] 56/S8/F3

C2719  angular intensity attenuation factor (angular attenuation factor)  [defined; defined_in: 56/S8; 2 of 2 facts shown]
  An angular intensity attenuation factor is a factor in a lighting model that, in its commonly used form, multiplies the sum of the diffuse and specular reflection components by zero for a surface point outside the angular limit of a spotlight cone and by the cosine of phi to a positive angular attenuation exponent inside it.
  built from: [1] 56/S8/F3, [2] 56/S8/F4

C2720  angular limit (theta)  [defined; defined_in: 56/S8; 1 of 1 facts shown]
  Angular limit is the range of the spotlight cone, outside of which a surface point's angular intensity attenuation factor is zero.
  built from: [1] 56/S8/F3

C2721  angular attenuation exponent (A1)  [defined; defined_in: 56/S8; 1 of 1 facts shown]
  An angular attenuation exponent (A1) is a positive exponent used in a spotlight's angular intensity attenuation factor, specifically the power to which the cosine of phi is raised for a surface point within the spotlight cone's angular limit.
  built from: [1] 56/S8/F3

C2722  intensity component  [not defined; defined_in: none; 1 of 1 facts shown]
  Intensity component is the I_M component of the simple lighting model modified for radial and angular intensity attenuation, and it is left unchanged.
  built from: [1] 56/S8/F4

C2723  colored light source model  [defined; defined_in: 56/S9; 3 of 3 facts shown]
  A colored light source model is a model in which emitted light intensity is a three-element red, green, and blue vector rather than a single component, and its components are computed individually and combined to give the perception of any color.
  built from: [1] 56/S9/F0, [2] 56/S9/F1

C2724  source intensity (emitted light intensity)  [defined; defined_in: 56/S9; 2 of 2 facts shown]
  Source intensity is a three-element vector in a colored light source model, with individual red, green, and blue components whose combination gives the perception of any color.

C2725  vector (three-element vector)  [not defined; defined_in: none; 6 of 6 facts shown]
  A vector is used to represent three components or coordinates, such as the red, green, and blue components of light intensity or the position of a 3D point.

C2726  single-light-source lighting model  [not defined; defined_in: none; 1 of 1 facts shown]
  A single-light-source lighting model is a lighting model that, for n light sources, gives the light at a point as ambient light plus each light source's contribution, calculated separately and added, with red, green, and blue components computed separately for colored light sources.
  built from: [1] 56/S9/F3

C2727  colored light source (colored light sources)  [not defined; defined_in: none; 1 of 1 facts shown]

C2728  intensity mapping  [defined; defined_in: 56/S10; 2 of 2 facts shown]
  Intensity mapping is the mapping in which a lighting-model pixel intensity, which can be any value in an infinite range between 0 and 1, is mapped to the nearest finite device-supported table intensity, so the corresponding bit pattern is stored in the frame buffer.
  built from: [1] 56/S10/F0, [2] 56/S10/F4

C2729  8-bit display  [defined; defined_in: none; 1 of 1 facts shown]

  checks: defined_mismatch

C2730  device-supported intensity value (intensity value, device-supported intensity level, intensity level)  [defined; defined_in: 56/S10; 5 of 5 facts shown]
  Device-supported intensity value is one of the finite intensity levels supported by a display, determined from minimum and maximum values by successive multiplication by a factor r, because humans perceive relative changes, and used to map lighting-model pixel intensities to frame-buffer bit patterns.
  built from: [1] 56/S10/F0, [2] 56/S10/F1, [3] 56/S10/F2, [4] 56/S10/F3, [5] 56/S10/F4

C2731  minimum device-supported intensity value (I0)  [defined; defined_in: 56/S10; 2 of 2 facts shown]

  built from: [1] 56/S10/F1, [2] 56/S10/F2

C2732  photometer  [not defined; defined_in: none; 1 of 1 facts shown]
  A Photometer is used to measure the minimum device-supported intensity value.
  built from: [1] 56/S10/F1

C2733  maximum intensity value  [not defined; defined_in: none; 1 of 1 facts shown]
  The maximum intensity value is the known intensity value, 1, used in the determination of device-supported intensity values.
  built from: [1] 56/S10/F1

C2734  multiplicative factor (r)  [defined; defined_in: 56/S10; 2 of 2 facts shown]
  Multiplicative factor is a factor used to calculate successive device-supported intensity values from an initial intensity, based on human perception of relative rather than absolute intensity changes.
  built from: [1] 56/S10/F2, [2] 56/S10/F3

C2735  psychophysiological property  [defined; defined_in: 56/S10; 1 of 1 facts shown]

  built from: [1] 56/S10/F3

C2736  relative change  [defined; defined_in: none; 1 of 1 facts shown]
  Relative change is a change between two intensity values, rather than an absolute change in intensity values, that humans perceive.
  built from: [1] 56/S10/F3
  checks: defined_mismatch

C2737  absolute change  [not defined; defined_in: none; 1 of 1 facts shown]

  built from: [1] 56/S10/F3

C2738  table  [defined; defined_in: 56/S10; 1 of 1 facts shown]
  A Table is a store that holds calculated device-supported intensity values and their assigned bit patterns, used to map a lighting-model pixel intensity to the nearest stored intensity and the corresponding bit pattern.
  built from: [1] 56/S10/F4

C2739  bit pattern  [not defined; defined_in: none; 1 of 1 facts shown]

  built from: [1] 56/S10/F4

C2740  two-dimensional display (2D display)  [not defined; defined_in: none; 1 of 1 facts shown]

C2741  three-dimensional viewing transformation (3D viewing transformation, 3D transformation)  [defined; defined_in: 57/S1; 2 of 2 facts shown]

After a view coordinate system is defined from camera parameters, it is performed by translation and rotation using a composite transformation matrix.

C2742  camera viewing mechanism  [not defined; defined_in: none; 1 of 1 facts shown]

  built from: [1] 57/S1/F1

C2743  camera parameters  [defined; defined_in: 57/S1; 2 of 2 facts shown]

C2754  view volume  [defined; defined_in: 57/S2; 9 of 9 facts shown]
  A view volume is a three-dimensional region in the scene that defines the portion of the scene projected onto the view plane; objects inside it are projected, and objects outside it are discarded. For parallel projection it is a rectangular parallelepiped, and for perspective projection a frustum, with one face or side serving as the clipping window.
  built from: [1] 57/S2/F2, [3] 57/S2/F6

C2755  perspective projection  [defined; defined_in: 57/S2; 5 of 5 facts shown]
  Perspective projection is a projection type in which projectors from an object in a three-dimensional view coordinate system to the view plane are not parallel but converge at a center of projection; the view volume is a frustum, and realistic rendering is produced for realistic scenes.
  built from: [1] 57/S2/F4, [2] 57/S2/F6

C2756  center of projection (point of projection)  [defined; defined_in: 57/S2; 3 of 3 facts shown]
  A center of projection is the point where projectors from an object in the 3D view coordinate system to the view plane converge in perspective projection, or the point at infinity in parallel projection.
  built from: [1] 57/S2/F4, [2] 57/S2/F5

C2757  parallel projection  [defined; defined_in: 57/S2; 6 of 6 facts shown]
  Parallel projection is a projection type in which the projectors from an object to the view plane are parallel to each other, so their center of projection is at infinity and they never meet.
  built from: [1] 57/S2/F5

C2758  frustum  [defined; defined_in: none; 3 of 3 facts shown]
  A frustum is the view volume for perspective projection, with one face or side serving as the clipping window on which objects inside it are projected.
  built from: [1] 57/S2/F6
  checks: defined_mismatch

C2759  naive clipping method  [defined; defined_in: 57/S2; 1 of 1 facts shown]
  The naive clipping method is a method that computes the intersection of view volume boundaries with object boundaries, a calculation that is computationally intensive for arbitrarily shaped view volumes such as frustums.
  built from: [1] 57/S2/F7

C2760  canonical view volume (unit cube)  [defined; defined_in: 57/S2; 4 of 4 facts shown]

  built from: [1] 57/S2/F8

C2761  point vector  [not defined; defined_in: none; 2 of 2 facts shown]

C2762  parallel projection matrix  [defined; defined_in: 57/S2; 2 of 2 facts shown]
  A parallel projection matrix is a four-by-four matrix, in which D is the distance of the clipping window from the origin, that is used to compute a parallel projection by multiplying a point vector in the 3D view coordinate system and dividing the obtained homogeneous coordinate values by w, typically not 1, to obtain Cartesian x, y, and z values.
  built from: [1] 57/S2/F11

C2763  origin  [not defined; defined_in: none; 2 of 2 facts shown]
  The origin is a point in the three-dimensional (3D) view coordinate system that is used for the distance of the clipping window and is typically assumed to be the center of projection.
  built from: [1] 57/S2/F11, [2] 57/S2/F12

C2764  homogeneous coordinate value  [not defined; defined_in: none; 2 of 2 facts shown]
  A homogeneous coordinate value is a coordinate value obtained when a 3D point vector is multiplied by a parallel projection matrix or a perspective projection matrix, and it is divided by the homogeneous factor w to obtain Cartesian coordinates.
  built from: [1] 57/S2/F11, [2] 57/S2/F12

C2765  homogeneous factor (w)  [defined; defined_in: none; 2 of 2 facts shown]
  The homogeneous factor is a factor, typically not equal to 1, by which obtained homogeneous coordinate values are divided to obtain Cartesian coordinates in parallel and perspective projection.
  built from: [1] 57/S2/F11, [2] 57/S2/F12
  checks: defined_mismatch

C2766  cartesian coordinate  [not defined; defined_in: none; 2 of 2 facts shown]
  A Cartesian coordinate is one of the x, y, or z values obtained by dividing homogeneous coordinate values by the homogeneous factor w when computing a projection of a point vector in a three-dimensional view coordinate system.
  built from: [1] 57/S2/F11, [2] 57/S2/F12

C2767  perspective projection matrix  [defined; defined_in: 57/S2; 1 of 1 facts shown]
  A Perspective projection matrix is a matrix used to compute a perspective projection by multiplying a point vector in the 3D view coordinate system, where D is the distance from the clipping window to the center of projection, typically assumed at the origin; the obtained homogeneous coordinate values are then divided by the homogeneous factor w.
  built from: [1] 57/S2/F12

C2768  viewport (2D viewport)  [defined; defined_in: 57/S3; 5 of 5 facts shown]
  A viewport is a portion of the final screen in device space, not on the view plane, where a scene is rendered, defined relative to the display origin and dimensions in a continuous coordinate system.

C2770  window point  [defined; defined_in: none; 2 of 2 facts shown]

C2771  viewport point  [defined; defined_in: none; 2 of 2 facts shown]

  checks: defined_mismatch

C2772  window-to-viewport transformation matrix (transformation matrix)  [defined; defined_in: 57/S3; 2 of 2 facts shown]
  The Window-to-viewport transformation matrix is a three-by-three matrix whose Sx and Sy are defined in terms of window and viewport dimensions, and whose Tx and Ty are computed from Sx, Sy, and those dimensions. Multiplying a window point vector by the matrix yields a homogeneous coordinate representation, whose Cartesian point is obtained by dividing by the homogeneous factor W.
  built from: [1] 57/S3/F3, [2] 57/S3/F4

  checks: defined_mismatch
